    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep having these nagging thoughts about my work — I’m a designer, so it’s usually things like, “What if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout?” or “What if I sent the wrong version to the client?” And it’s not always something big or obvious. Sometimes it’s like a spacing issue, or the wrong file, or a little typo I’m worried I somehow didn’t catch.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can look at it and know, rationally, it’s probably fine. Like, I can see the file, I can see what I sent, and part of me knows there’s no real reason to keep thinking about it. But my mind just keeps looping back to it anyway, like it won’t quite let go. It’s mild, I guess, compared to what some people deal with, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking your work multiple times, to try and make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s mostly around submitting work. Before I send something out now, I’ll re-open the file even if I already checked it, and I’ll look through the layers again, compare what’s visible, make sure nothing weird is hidden or accidentally moved. Then I’ll sometimes re-save it, even though I know I just saved it, almost like I need to make the action feel “settled,” if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this mental walkthrough of the design choices — like, “Okay, that spacing was intentional, that color was the approved one, that version is the final version,” and I’ll kind of retrace the whole thing in my head one more time. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it does slow me down. And honestly, it makes the work feel less free, less creative, because instead of just trusting my eye, I’m sort of auditing myself over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s completely derailing my life or anything. I’m still getting the work done, clients are still receiving things, and from the outside I probably look functional. But it is starting to have consequences in a way that worries me.\n\nI’ve missed a few internal deadlines — not huge client-facing disasters, more like the team deadline before something goes out — because I’ll keep holding onto a file longer than I should, checking it again, or hesitating to call it finished. And that’s frustrating because I know I’m capable of working faster than that.\n\nThe other part is maybe more upsetting to me, honestly. I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with design choices. Like, if I have a bolder idea, something more visually interesting or a little riskier, I’ll sometimes talk myself out of it because then there are more things to justify, more chances I’ll miss some little issue, or more ways it could be questioned. And I’m at a point in my career where I really want to move toward a creative director role, so I feel like I should be showing that kind of confidence and vision. Instead, I’m sometimes shrinking my work down to what feels safest.\n\nSo… yes, it’s mild, I guess, but it’s distressing because it’s starting to affect the kind of designer I’m being, not just how long I spend checking a file.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations or interactions with colleagues because of your worries about your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m really avoiding people, no. At least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed, like skipping meetings or refusing to talk to colleagues. I still interact with the team, I still discuss projects, I still respond when people ask me things.\n\nI guess sometimes I might feel a little hesitant before showing work, especially if I’m not fully “settled” on whether I’ve checked it enough, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social situations because of it. It’s more internal, like I’m in my own head about the work, rather than staying away from people. If anything, I probably just take longer before I share something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about work are significantly impacting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I keep wanting to qualify it because I’m not, like, unable to function. I am still working, I’m still finishing projects, I’m still showing up and doing the day-to-day things. So part of me feels almost dramatic saying it’s “significant.”\n\nBut it is affecting me. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I can’t quite let go of the work when I should. I’ll hold onto a file longer than necessary because I feel like I need to check it one more time, or make sure I haven’t missed some small thing that could make me look careless. And even if it’s not a huge public failure, it creates this pressure, because those internal timelines matter to the team.\n\nAnd the bigger thing for my well-being is that I feel like it’s changing how I create. I’ve noticed myself backing away from bolder design ideas — things I might normally be excited to try — because they feel like they come with more risk, more chances for something to be questioned or for me to overlook something. That really bothers me because I’m trying to grow toward a creative director role, and I know I need to be more confident and visionary, not constantly playing it safe just to avoid anxiety.\n\nSo, yes, I’m functioning, but I don’t feel as free or confident in my work as I used to. And that’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and checking behaviors are in line with your own values and how you want to approach your work?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most about it, because it feels like it’s pulling me away from how I want to work.\n\nI care about doing careful, polished work — that part is in line with my values. I don’t want to be sloppy, and I don’t want to send something out with obvious mistakes. But the checking has gone past that normal professional standard, I think. It’s not just “I’m being thorough.” It’s more like I’m holding onto things because I’m scared that if I miss one small issue, it’ll reflect badly on me or make people question whether I’m ready for the next step.\n\nAnd that’s where it feels really out of sync with who I want to be as a designer. I want to be more decisive, more creative, more willing to take a strong point of view. Especially because I’m hoping to move toward a creative director role, and I know that requires confidence and judgment — not just making sure every tiny thing is safe. But lately I’ll catch myself choosing the less risky design option, not because I think it’s better, but because it feels easier to defend or less likely to have some hidden problem I overlooked.\n\nSo I’m still getting my work done, but it doesn’t feel like I’m approaching it the way I want to. Missing internal deadlines because I keep checking, and holding back on bolder ideas because I’m afraid of damaging my chances… that feels really frustrating. It feels like the worry is making decisions for me sometimes, instead of my actual creative instincts.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries or checking behaviors are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to anything major, honestly. It’s much more centered around work, or at least that’s where I notice it the most because that’s where the stakes feel clearer to me.\n\nI mean, I probably do little checking things in normal life like anyone does — making sure I have my keys, making sure I sent a message to the right person, rereading something before I send it — but I don’t think it causes me significant distress in the same way. It doesn’t feel like it’s taking over my evenings or making me avoid things outside of work, if that makes sense.\n\nThe distress is really tied to my job and the sense that a mistake could affect how people see me professionally, or whether I’m ready for more responsibility. Outside of that, I haven’t noticed the same pattern causing a big problem. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these nagging thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure. It’s hard for me to put an exact start date on it, because it didn’t feel like something that suddenly appeared one day. It’s more like… at some point I realized I was spending more time circling back over things than I used to.\n\nI can say it’s been long enough that it feels like a pattern now, not just one stressful week or one particular project. But if you asked me, like, “Was it six months, a year, two years?” I don’t know that I could answer confidently without really thinking back through specific work periods.\n\nI think I noticed it gradually — first as being extra careful, then maybe being “thorough,” and then eventually realizing, okay, I’m not just checking because it’s useful, I’m checking because I feel unsettled if I don’t. But I don’t have a clean timeline for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your relationships with friends or family due to your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not in a big, obvious way, I don’t think. I haven’t had anyone say, “You’re not present,” or “You’re always distracted by work,” or anything like that. My friends and family aren’t really involved in the checking itself, because that mostly happens when I’m working.\n\nI guess there are moments where I’m mentally still at work, like if I’m thinking about whether I missed something or whether something I sent was okay, I might be a little less relaxed or a little quieter. But I don’t know that I’d say it’s damaged my relationships. It’s more internal for me — like I’m carrying that worry around — but I don’t think it’s caused major conflict or anything.\n\nSo… I’d say maybe it affects my mood sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a significant relationship impact.\n\nDoctor: Are there any work tasks or projects that you've been putting off or avoiding altogether because of your fear of making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole projects altogether, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting my work done, so it’s not like I’m refusing assignments or just not turning things in. But… yes, there are definitely tasks where I sort of slow down or put off moving them forward because I’m worried I’ll miss something.\n\nIt happens especially with pieces that feel more visible, or where there’s more room for judgment — like design concepts that are supposed to be a little more ambitious. I’ll start second-guessing whether the idea is too risky, whether I’ve overlooked some detail, whether someone will catch something and think, “Why didn’t she notice that?” And then instead of pushing the stronger or more interesting direction, I’ll sometimes stay safer than I probably need to.\n\nI’ve also started missing some internal deadlines, not client deadlines so much, but the check-in points or the timelines I set with the team. It’s frustrating because I’m not missing them because I’m careless — it’s almost the opposite. I’m spending too much time reviewing and adjusting and making sure nothing is wrong, and then I’m late anyway.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me most is that I’m trying to move toward more leadership, eventually a creative director role, and I worry that if I make mistakes now, people will see me as not ready. But then the checking and hesitating kind of creates its own problem, because I’m not always showing the boldness or confidence I know that role would require.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to function effectively in your job or in social situations is significantly impaired because of these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s significantly impairing me socially. Socially, it’s more that I might be distracted or still thinking about work, but I’m not avoiding people or having major problems with friends or family because of it.\n\nAt work, though… yes, there is some impact. I’m still functioning, and I’m still getting my actual work done, so part of me hesitates to call it “significant,” because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or unable to do my job. But it is interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe main thing is that I’ve started missing internal deadlines — not usually the final client deadlines, but the check-ins, the drafts, the points where I’m supposed to show progress to the team. And it’s because I keep circling back, reviewing things, tweaking, checking for mistakes, trying to make sure I haven’t overlooked something. So I’m trying to be careful, but then the process gets slower and heavier than it should.\n\nAnd I also notice I hold back on bolder design ideas sometimes. Like if there’s a concept that could be stronger or more interesting, I’ll start thinking, “What if there’s some flaw I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” And because I’m trying to move toward more of a creative director role eventually, I worry that a visible mistake would make people think I’m not ready. So yes, I’m still doing my job, but I do think these worries are affecting how confidently and efficiently I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to your work or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not that I’ve noticed in the same way. The thoughts that feel intrusive are pretty much tied to work, especially layouts or client files.\n\nIt’s like I’ll get this nagging thought that I missed some tiny mistake, or that I sent the wrong version to a client, even when I can look at it and think, “Okay, logically this is probably fine.” But then my mind doesn’t really let it go. It keeps circling back to it, like there’s this little hook in my brain saying, “Are you sure? What if you didn’t check that one part?” \n\nBut outside of that work mistake theme, I can’t think of other recurring intrusive thoughts that stand out. It’s really mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your personal life where you feel like you're not enjoying things as much as you used to because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m not enjoying my personal life as much, exactly. I mean, I still enjoy things when I’m actually present for them — dinner with my husband, seeing friends, watching something, going for a walk, that kind of thing. It’s not like everything feels flat or pointless.\n\nBut sometimes the work worries sort of follow me home, if that makes sense. I’ll be doing something normal and then I’ll get pulled back into thinking about whether I missed something or whether I should check something again. So in those moments I’m less relaxed than I want to be. It’s more like I’m distracted or preoccupied, not that I’ve lost interest in my life.\n\nI don’t think there’s a whole area of my personal life that I’ve stopped enjoying. It’s more intermittent — like the worry intrudes on my attention, and then I have to work to come back to whatever I’m doing. So… no, not in a major way, but it does take some of the ease out of things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies outside of work that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I’m trying to think through it because I don’t want to just automatically say no if there’s some pattern I haven’t noticed.\n\nI still do the things I normally like doing outside of work. I still spend time with my husband, I still see friends, I still go out or do low-key things at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped a hobby because of the worries. It’s more that sometimes I’m mentally not as fully there as I’d like to be, but I’m still doing the activities.\n\nLike, if anything is happening less often, it’s probably more because of normal life and being busy or tired, not because I’m avoiding it due to the work worries. I don’t think there’s a specific hobby where I’d say, “I gave that up because I was too worried about checking things.” It’s more occasional distraction than actually stopping activities.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your overall quality of life is being significantly impacted by these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say my whole quality of life is significantly impacted, like I’m falling apart or not functioning. I am still getting my work done, and from the outside I probably look like I’m handling things pretty normally.\n\nBut… yes, there is an impact, especially professionally. It’s not just annoying thoughts anymore. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I spend too long checking or second-guessing things before I’m willing to send them along. And I know those internal deadlines aren’t the same as a client deadline, but they still matter. People notice if I’m slower or if I’m holding things up.\n\nThe other part that bothers me is that I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with my design choices. Like, I’ll have a bolder idea, something more creative or maybe more leadership-level, and then I talk myself out of it because I’m afraid there’ll be something I overlooked or some flaw I didn’t catch. So I’ll choose the cleaner, safer option because it feels less risky.\n\nAnd that worries me because I do want to move toward a creative director role eventually. I don’t want to be seen as careless, obviously, but I also don’t want to become someone who’s so cautious that I stop taking creative risks. So I guess it’s mild in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it’s affecting the way I work and the way I see my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries are causing you significant distress or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I don’t think it’s causing significant distress in the same way. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself if I’m trying to relax and my mind drifts back to work, but I wouldn’t say it’s making it hard for me to do the things I need or want to do in my personal life.\n\nI’m still going out, still spending time with my husband, still keeping up with normal life stuff. It doesn’t feel like I’m avoiding personal activities because of the worry. It’s more like the worry sometimes tags along in the background, which is frustrating, but not disabling.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not significantly outside of work. And with friends and family, I’m still showing up. I still text people back, I still make plans, I still enjoy being with them once I’m there.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not suffering. Like if there’s a concept that could be stronger or more interesting, I’ll start thinking, “What if there’s some flaw I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” And because I’m trying to move toward more of a creative director role eventually, I worry that a visible mistake would make people think I’m not ready. So yes, I’m still doing my job, but I do think these worries are affecting how confidently and efficiently I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to your work or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not that I’ve noticed in the same way. The thoughts that feel intrusive are pretty much tied to work, especially layouts or client files.\n\nIt’s like I’ll get this nagging thought that I missed some tiny mistake, or that I sent the wrong version to a client, even when I can look at it and think, “Okay, logically this is probably fine.” But then my mind doesn’t really let it go. It keeps circling back to it, like there’s this little hook in my brain saying, “Are you sure? What if you didn’t check that one part?” \n\nBut outside of that work mistake theme, I can’t think of other recurring intrusive thoughts that stand out. It’s really mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your personal life where you feel like you're not enjoying things as much as you used to because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m not enjoying my personal life as much, exactly. I mean, I still enjoy things when I’m actually present for them — dinner with my husband, seeing friends, watching something, going for a walk, that kind of thing. It’s not like everything feels flat or pointless.\n\nBut sometimes the work worries sort of follow me home, if that makes sense. I’ll be doing something normal and then I’ll get pulled back into thinking about whether I missed something or whether I should check something again. So in those moments I’m less relaxed than I want to be. It’s more like I’m distracted or preoccupied, not that I’ve lost interest in my life.\n\nI don’t think there’s a whole area of my personal life that I’ve stopped enjoying. It’s more intermittent — like the worry intrudes on my attention, and then I have to work to come back to whatever I’m doing. So… no, not in a major way, but it does take some of the ease out of things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies outside of work that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I’m trying to think through it because I don’t want to just automatically say no if there’s some pattern I haven’t noticed.\n\nI still do the things I normally like doing outside of work. I still spend time with my husband, I still see friends, I still go out or do low-key things at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped a hobby because of the worries. It’s more that sometimes I’m mentally not as fully there as I’d like to be, but I’m still doing the activities.\n\nLike, if anything is happening less often, it’s probably more because of normal life and being busy or tired, not because I’m avoiding it due to the work worries. I don’t think there’s a specific hobby where I’d say, “I gave that up because I was too worried about checking things.” It’s more occasional distraction than actually stopping activities.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your overall quality of life is being significantly impacted by these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say my whole quality of life is significantly impacted, like I’m falling apart or not functioning. I am still getting my work done, and from the outside I probably look like I’m handling things pretty normally.\n\nBut… yes, there is an impact, especially professionally. It’s not just annoying thoughts anymore. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I spend too long checking or second-guessing things before I’m willing to send them along. And I know those internal deadlines aren’t the same as a client deadline, but they still matter. People notice if I’m slower or if I’m holding things up.\n\nThe other part that bothers me is that I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with my design choices. Like, I’ll have a bolder idea, something more creative or maybe more leadership-level, and then I talk myself out of it because I’m afraid there’ll be something I overlooked or some flaw I didn’t catch. So I’ll choose the cleaner, safer option because it feels less risky.\n\nAnd that worries me because I do want to move toward a creative director role eventually. I don’t want to be seen as careless, obviously, but I also don’t want to become someone who’s so cautious that I stop taking creative risks. So I guess it’s mild in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it’s affecting the way I work and the way I see my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries are causing you significant distress or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I don’t think it’s causing significant distress in the same way. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself if I’m trying to relax and my mind drifts back to work, but I wouldn’t say it’s making it hard for me to do the things I need or want to do in my personal life.\n\nI’m still going out, still spending time with my husband, still keeping up with normal life stuff. It doesn’t feel like I’m avoiding personal activities because of the worry. It’s more like the worry sometimes tags along in the background, which is frustrating, but not disabling.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not significantly outside of work. It’s mostly the work side where I notice it actually affecting my choices and pace.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with your husband, friends, or family are suffering because of your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they’re really suffering, no. At least not in a way that I’ve noticed, and my husband hasn’t said anything like, “You’re not present,” or “This is becoming a problem.” I mean, I’m sure there are moments where I’m more distracted than I’d like to be, especially if something at work is still kind of sitting in my head, but I don’t think it’s damaging the relationships.\n\nWith my husband, I might talk something through more than I need to sometimes, or ask for reassurance in a kind of roundabout way, but it’s not like we’re fighting about it or I’m withdrawing from him. And with friends and family, I’m still showing up. I still text people back, I still make plans, I still enjoy being with them once I’m there.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not suffering. Maybe occasionally I’m not as mentally available as I’d like, but I haven’t seen it create real strain in those relationships.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Outside of work, not really in a significant way. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about things, but when I think about actual interference — like not being able to do normal life things or avoiding situations — I don’t really see that happening much outside of my job.

The main place it shows up is still work. I’m technically getting my work done, so I don’t want to overstate it like I’m completely unable to function, because that wouldn’t be true. But I have started missing internal deadlines because I get caught checking and rechecking or hesitating before I send something along. And those aren’t always huge deadlines, but they do affect the flow of projects, and I worry people will start to see me as slow or overly cautious.

And then the other thing is that it’s affecting my choices creatively. I’ll have an idea that’s stronger or more interesting, and then I start thinking, “What if there’s something wrong with it that I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” So I back away from it and choose something safer. That part bothers me a lot, because I want to keep growing. I’d like to move toward a creative director role someday, and I’m afraid this pattern could hold me back.

So outside of work, I’d say no, not significantly. But professionally, yes — it’s starting to interfere, even if I’m still managing overall.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep having these nagging thoughts about my work — I’m a designer, so it’s usually things like, “What if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout?” or “What if I sent the wrong version to the client?” And it’s not always something big or obvious. Sometimes it’s like a spacing issue, or the wrong file, or a little typo I’m worried I somehow didn’t catch.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can look at it and know, rationally, it’s probably fine. Like, I can see the file, I can see what I sent, and part of me knows there’s no real reason to keep thinking about it. But my mind just keeps looping back to it anyway, like it won’t quite let go. It’s mild, I guess, compared to what some people deal with, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking your work multiple times, to try and make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s mostly around submitting work. Before I send something out now, I’ll re-open the file even if I already checked it, and I’ll look through the layers again, compare what’s visible, make sure nothing weird is hidden or accidentally moved. Then I’ll sometimes re-save it, even though I know I just saved it, almost like I need to make the action feel “settled,” if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this mental walkthrough of the design choices — like, “Okay, that spacing was intentional, that color was the approved one, that version is the final version,” and I’ll kind of retrace the whole thing in my head one more time. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it does slow me down. And honestly, it makes the work feel less free, less creative, because instead of just trusting my eye, I’m sort of auditing myself over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s completely derailing my life or anything. I’m still getting the work done, clients are still receiving things, and from the outside I probably look functional. But it is starting to have consequences in a way that worries me.\n\nI’ve missed a few internal deadlines — not huge client-facing disasters, more like the team deadline before something goes out — because I’ll keep holding onto a file longer than I should, checking it again, or hesitating to call it finished. And that’s frustrating because I know I’m capable of working faster than that.\n\nThe other part is maybe more upsetting to me, honestly. I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with design choices. Like, if I have a bolder idea, something more visually interesting or a little riskier, I’ll sometimes talk myself out of it because then there are more things to justify, more chances I’ll miss some little issue, or more ways it could be questioned. And I’m at a point in my career where I really want to move toward a creative director role, so I feel like I should be showing that kind of confidence and vision. Instead, I’m sometimes shrinking my work down to what feels safest.\n\nSo… yes, it’s mild, I guess, but it’s distressing because it’s starting to affect the kind of designer I’m being, not just how long I spend checking a file.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations or interactions with colleagues because of your worries about your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m really avoiding people, no. At least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed, like skipping meetings or refusing to talk to colleagues. I still interact with the team, I still discuss projects, I still respond when people ask me things.\n\nI guess sometimes I might feel a little hesitant before showing work, especially if I’m not fully “settled” on whether I’ve checked it enough, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social situations because of it. It’s more internal, like I’m in my own head about the work, rather than staying away from people. If anything, I probably just take longer before I share something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about work are significantly impacting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I keep wanting to qualify it because I’m not, like, unable to function. I am still working, I’m still finishing projects, I’m still showing up and doing the day-to-day things. So part of me feels almost dramatic saying it’s “significant.”\n\nBut it is affecting me. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I can’t quite let go of the work when I should. I’ll hold onto a file longer than necessary because I feel like I need to check it one more time, or make sure I haven’t missed some small thing that could make me look careless. And even if it’s not a huge public failure, it creates this pressure, because those internal timelines matter to the team.\n\nAnd the bigger thing for my well-being is that I feel like it’s changing how I create. I’ve noticed myself backing away from bolder design ideas — things I might normally be excited to try — because they feel like they come with more risk, more chances for something to be questioned or for me to overlook something. That really bothers me because I’m trying to grow toward a creative director role, and I know I need to be more confident and visionary, not constantly playing it safe just to avoid anxiety.\n\nSo, yes, I’m functioning, but I don’t feel as free or confident in my work as I used to. And that’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and checking behaviors are in line with your own values and how you want to approach your work?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most about it, because it feels like it’s pulling me away from how I want to work.\n\nI care about doing careful, polished work — that part is in line with my values. I don’t want to be sloppy, and I don’t want to send something out with obvious mistakes. But the checking has gone past that normal professional standard, I think. It’s not just “I’m being thorough.” It’s more like I’m holding onto things because I’m scared that if I miss one small issue, it’ll reflect badly on me or make people question whether I’m ready for the next step.\n\nAnd that’s where it feels really out of sync with who I want to be as a designer. I want to be more decisive, more creative, more willing to take a strong point of view. Especially because I’m hoping to move toward a creative director role, and I know that requires confidence and judgment — not just making sure every tiny thing is safe. But lately I’ll catch myself choosing the less risky design option, not because I think it’s better, but because it feels easier to defend or less likely to have some hidden problem I overlooked.\n\nSo I’m still getting my work done, but it doesn’t feel like I’m approaching it the way I want to. Missing internal deadlines because I keep checking, and holding back on bolder ideas because I’m afraid of damaging my chances… that feels really frustrating. It feels like the worry is making decisions for me sometimes, instead of my actual creative instincts.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries or checking behaviors are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to anything major, honestly. It’s much more centered around work, or at least that’s where I notice it the most because that’s where the stakes feel clearer to me.\n\nI mean, I probably do little checking things in normal life like anyone does — making sure I have my keys, making sure I sent a message to the right person, rereading something before I send it — but I don’t think it causes me significant distress in the same way. It doesn’t feel like it’s taking over my evenings or making me avoid things outside of work, if that makes sense.\n\nThe distress is really tied to my job and the sense that a mistake could affect how people see me professionally, or whether I’m ready for more responsibility. Outside of that, I haven’t noticed the same pattern causing a big problem. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these nagging thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure. It’s hard for me to put an exact start date on it, because it didn’t feel like something that suddenly appeared one day. It’s more like… at some point I realized I was spending more time circling back over things than I used to.\n\nI can say it’s been long enough that it feels like a pattern now, not just one stressful week or one particular project. But if you asked me, like, “Was it six months, a year, two years?” I don’t know that I could answer confidently without really thinking back through specific work periods.\n\nI think I noticed it gradually — first as being extra careful, then maybe being “thorough,” and then eventually realizing, okay, I’m not just checking because it’s useful, I’m checking because I feel unsettled if I don’t. But I don’t have a clean timeline for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your relationships with friends or family due to your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not in a big, obvious way, I don’t think. I haven’t had anyone say, “You’re not present,” or “You’re always distracted by work,” or anything like that. My friends and family aren’t really involved in the checking itself, because that mostly happens when I’m working.\n\nI guess there are moments where I’m mentally still at work, like if I’m thinking about whether I missed something or whether something I sent was okay, I might be a little less relaxed or a little quieter. But I don’t know that I’d say it’s damaged my relationships. It’s more internal for me — like I’m carrying that worry around — but I don’t think it’s caused major conflict or anything.\n\nSo… I’d say maybe it affects my mood sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a significant relationship impact.\n\nDoctor: Are there any work tasks or projects that you've been putting off or avoiding altogether because of your fear of making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole projects altogether, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting my work done, so it’s not like I’m refusing assignments or just not turning things in. But… yes, there are definitely tasks where I sort of slow down or put off moving them forward because I’m worried I’ll miss something.\n\nIt happens especially with pieces that feel more visible, or where there’s more room for judgment — like design concepts that are supposed to be a little more ambitious. I’ll start second-guessing whether the idea is too risky, whether I’ve overlooked some detail, whether someone will catch something and think, “Why didn’t she notice that?” And then instead of pushing the stronger or more interesting direction, I’ll sometimes stay safer than I probably need to.\n\nI’ve also started missing some internal deadlines, not client deadlines so much, but the check-in points or the timelines I set with the team. It’s frustrating because I’m not missing them because I’m careless — it’s almost the opposite. I’m spending too much time reviewing and adjusting and making sure nothing is wrong, and then I’m late anyway.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me most is that I’m trying to move toward more leadership, eventually a creative director role, and I worry that if I make mistakes now, people will see me as not ready. But then the checking and hesitating kind of creates its own problem, because I’m not always showing the boldness or confidence I know that role would require.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to function effectively in your job or in social situations is significantly impaired because of these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s significantly impairing me socially. Socially, it’s more that I might be distracted or still thinking about work, but I’m not avoiding people or having major problems with friends or family because of it.\n\nAt work, though… yes, there is some impact. I’m still functioning, and I’m still getting my actual work done, so part of me hesitates to call it “significant,” because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or unable to do my job. But it is interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe main thing is that I’ve started missing internal deadlines — not usually the final client deadlines, but the check-ins, the drafts, the points where I’m supposed to show progress to the team. And it’s because I keep circling back, reviewing things, tweaking, checking for mistakes, trying to make sure I haven’t overlooked something. So I’m trying to be careful, but then the process gets slower and heavier than it should.\n\nAnd I also notice I hold back on bolder design ideas sometimes. Like if there’s a concept that could be stronger or more interesting, I’ll start thinking, “What if there’s some flaw I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” And because I’m trying to move toward more of a creative director role eventually, I worry that a visible mistake would make people think I’m not ready. So yes, I’m still doing my job, but I do think these worries are affecting how confidently and efficiently I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to your work or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not that I’ve noticed in the same way. The thoughts that feel intrusive are pretty much tied to work, especially layouts or client files.\n\nIt’s like I’ll get this nagging thought that I missed some tiny mistake, or that I sent the wrong version to a client, even when I can look at it and think, “Okay, logically this is probably fine.” But then my mind doesn’t really let it go. It keeps circling back to it, like there’s this little hook in my brain saying, “Are you sure? What if you didn’t check that one part?” \n\nBut outside of that work mistake theme, I can’t think of other recurring intrusive thoughts that stand out. It’s really mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your personal life where you feel like you're not enjoying things as much as you used to because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m not enjoying my personal life as much, exactly. I mean, I still enjoy things when I’m actually present for them — dinner with my husband, seeing friends, watching something, going for a walk, that kind of thing. It’s not like everything feels flat or pointless.\n\nBut sometimes the work worries sort of follow me home, if that makes sense. I’ll be doing something normal and then I’ll get pulled back into thinking about whether I missed something or whether I should check something again. So in those moments I’m less relaxed than I want to be. It’s more like I’m distracted or preoccupied, not that I’ve lost interest in my life.\n\nI don’t think there’s a whole area of my personal life that I’ve stopped enjoying. It’s more intermittent — like the worry intrudes on my attention, and then I have to work to come back to whatever I’m doing. So… no, not in a major way, but it does take some of the ease out of things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies outside of work that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I’m trying to think through it because I don’t want to just automatically say no if there’s some pattern I haven’t noticed.\n\nI still do the things I normally like doing outside of work. I still spend time with my husband, I still see friends, I still go out or do low-key things at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped a hobby because of the worries. It’s more that sometimes I’m mentally not as fully there as I’d like to be, but I’m still doing the activities.\n\nLike, if anything is happening less often, it’s probably more because of normal life and being busy or tired, not because I’m avoiding it due to the work worries. I don’t think there’s a specific hobby where I’d say, “I gave that up because I was too worried about checking things.” It’s more occasional distraction than actually stopping activities.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your overall quality of life is being significantly impacted by these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say my whole quality of life is significantly impacted, like I’m falling apart or not functioning. I am still getting my work done, and from the outside I probably look like I’m handling things pretty normally.\n\nBut… yes, there is an impact, especially professionally. It’s not just annoying thoughts anymore. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I spend too long checking or second-guessing things before I’m willing to send them along. And I know those internal deadlines aren’t the same as a client deadline, but they still matter. People notice if I’m slower or if I’m holding things up.\n\nThe other part that bothers me is that I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with my design choices. Like, I’ll have a bolder idea, something more creative or maybe more leadership-level, and then I talk myself out of it because I’m afraid there’ll be something I overlooked or some flaw I didn’t catch. So I’ll choose the cleaner, safer option because it feels less risky.\n\nAnd that worries me because I do want to move toward a creative director role eventually. I don’t want to be seen as careless, obviously, but I also don’t want to become someone who’s so cautious that I stop taking creative risks. So I guess it’s mild in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it’s affecting the way I work and the way I see my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries are causing you significant distress or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I don’t think it’s causing significant distress in the same way. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself if I’m trying to relax and my mind drifts back to work, but I wouldn’t say it’s making it hard for me to do the things I need or want to do in my personal life.\n\nI’m still going out, still spending time with my husband, still keeping up with normal life stuff. It doesn’t feel like I’m avoiding personal activities because of the worry. It’s more like the worry sometimes tags along in the background, which is frustrating, but not disabling.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not significantly outside of work. It’s mostly the work side where I notice it actually affecting my choices and pace.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with your husband, friends, or family are suffering because of your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they’re really suffering, no. At least not in a way that I’ve noticed, and my husband hasn’t said anything like, “You’re not present,” or “This is becoming a problem.” I mean, I’m sure there are moments where I’m more distracted than I’d like to be, especially if something at work is still kind of sitting in my head, but I don’t think it’s damaging the relationships.\n\nWith my husband, I might talk something through more than I need to sometimes, or ask for reassurance in a kind of roundabout way, but it’s not like we’re fighting about it or I’m withdrawing from him. And with friends and family, I’m still showing up. I still text people back, I still make plans, I still enjoy being with them once I’m there.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not suffering. Maybe occasionally I’m not as mentally available as I’d like, but I haven’t seen it create real strain in those relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your worries or habits are causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, not really in a significant way. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about things, but when I think about actual interference — like not being able to do normal life things or avoiding situations — I don’t really see that happening much outside of my job.\n\nThe main place it shows up is still work. I’m technically getting my work done, so I don’t want to overstate it like I’m completely unable to function, because that wouldn’t be true. But I have started missing internal deadlines because I get caught checking and rechecking or hesitating before I send something along. And those aren’t always huge deadlines, but they do affect the flow of projects, and I worry people will start to see me as slow or overly cautious.\n\nAnd then the other thing is that it’s affecting my choices creatively. I’ll have an idea that’s stronger or more interesting, and then I start thinking, “What if there’s something wrong with it that I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” So I back away from it and choose something safer. That part bothers me a lot, because I want to keep growing. I’d like to move toward a creative director role someday, and I’m afraid this pattern could hold me back.\n\nSo outside of work, I’d say no, not significantly. But professionally, yes — it’s starting to interfere, even if I’m still managing overall.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I guess it’s mild in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it’s affecting the way I work and the way I see my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries are causing you significant distress or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I don’t think it’s causing significant distress in the same way. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself if I’m trying to relax and my mind drifts back to work, but I wouldn’t say it’s making it hard for me to do the things I need or want to do in my personal life.\n\nI’m still going out, still spending time with my husband, still keeping up with normal life stuff. It doesn’t feel like I’m avoiding personal activities because of the worry. It’s more like the worry sometimes tags along in the background, which is frustrating, but not disabling.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not significantly outside of work. It’s mostly the work side where I notice it actually affecting my choices and pace.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with your husband, friends, or family are suffering because of your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they’re really suffering, no. At least not in a way that I’ve noticed, and my husband hasn’t said anything like, “You’re not present,” or “This is becoming a problem.” I mean, I’m sure there are moments where I’m more distracted than I’d like to be, especially if something at work is still kind of sitting in my head, but I don’t think it’s damaging the relationships.\n\nWith my husband, I might talk something through more than I need to sometimes, or ask for reassurance in a kind of roundabout way, but it’s not like we’re fighting about it or I’m withdrawing from him. And with friends and family, I’m still showing up. I still text people back, I still make plans, I still enjoy being with them once I’m there.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not suffering. Maybe occasionally I’m not as mentally available as I’d like, but I haven’t seen it create real strain in those relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your worries or habits are causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, not really in a significant way. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about things, but when I think about actual interference — like not being able to do normal life things or avoiding situations — I don’t really see that happening much outside of my job.\n\nThe main place it shows up is still work. I’m technically getting my work done, so I don’t want to overstate it like I’m completely unable to function, because that wouldn’t be true. That part bothers me a lot, because I want to keep growing. I’d like to move toward a creative director role someday, and I’m afraid this pattern could hold me back.\n\nSo outside of work, I’d say no, not significantly.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I keep having these nagging thoughts about my work — I’m a designer, so it’s usually things like, “What if I missed some tiny mistake in the layout?” or “What if I sent the wrong version to the client?” And it’s not always something big or obvious. Sometimes it’s like a spacing issue, or the wrong file, or a little typo I’m worried I somehow didn’t catch.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can look at it and know, rationally, it’s probably fine. Like, I can see the file, I can see what I sent, and part of me knows there’s no real reason to keep thinking about it. But my mind just keeps looping back to it anyway, like it won’t quite let go. It’s mild, I guess, compared to what some people deal with, but it’s been bothering me enough that I thought I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking your work multiple times, to try and make these thoughts go away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s mostly around submitting work. Before I send something out now, I’ll re-open the file even if I already checked it, and I’ll look through the layers again, compare what’s visible, make sure nothing weird is hidden or accidentally moved. Then I’ll sometimes re-save it, even though I know I just saved it, almost like I need to make the action feel “settled,” if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then I’ll do this mental walkthrough of the design choices — like, “Okay, that spacing was intentional, that color was the approved one, that version is the final version,” and I’ll kind of retrace the whole thing in my head one more time. It doesn’t take over my whole day or anything, but it does slow me down. And honestly, it makes the work feel less free, less creative, because instead of just trusting my eye, I’m sort of auditing myself over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these checking behaviors are interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s completely derailing my life or anything. I’m still getting the work done, clients are still receiving things, and from the outside I probably look functional. But it is starting to have consequences in a way that worries me.\n\nI’ve missed a few internal deadlines — not huge client-facing disasters, more like the team deadline before something goes out — because I’ll keep holding onto a file longer than I should, checking it again, or hesitating to call it finished. And that’s frustrating because I know I’m capable of working faster than that.\n\nThe other part is maybe more upsetting to me, honestly. I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with design choices. Like, if I have a bolder idea, something more visually interesting or a little riskier, I’ll sometimes talk myself out of it because then there are more things to justify, more chances I’ll miss some little issue, or more ways it could be questioned. And I’m at a point in my career where I really want to move toward a creative director role, so I feel like I should be showing that kind of confidence and vision. Instead, I’m sometimes shrinking my work down to what feels safest.\n\nSo… yes, it’s mild, I guess, but it’s distressing because it’s starting to affect the kind of designer I’m being, not just how long I spend checking a file.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations or interactions with colleagues because of your worries about your work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’m really avoiding people, no. At least not in a clear way that I’ve noticed, like skipping meetings or refusing to talk to colleagues. I still interact with the team, I still discuss projects, I still respond when people ask me things.\n\nI guess sometimes I might feel a little hesitant before showing work, especially if I’m not fully “settled” on whether I’ve checked it enough, but I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding social situations because of it. It’s more internal, like I’m in my own head about the work, rather than staying away from people. If anything, I probably just take longer before I share something.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries about work are significantly impacting your overall well-being or ability to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so, though I keep wanting to qualify it because I’m not, like, unable to function. I am still working, I’m still finishing projects, I’m still showing up and doing the day-to-day things. So part of me feels almost dramatic saying it’s “significant.”\n\nBut it is affecting me. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I can’t quite let go of the work when I should. I’ll hold onto a file longer than necessary because I feel like I need to check it one more time, or make sure I haven’t missed some small thing that could make me look careless. And even if it’s not a huge public failure, it creates this pressure, because those internal timelines matter to the team.\n\nAnd the bigger thing for my well-being is that I feel like it’s changing how I create. I’ve noticed myself backing away from bolder design ideas — things I might normally be excited to try — because they feel like they come with more risk, more chances for something to be questioned or for me to overlook something. That really bothers me because I’m trying to grow toward a creative director role, and I know I need to be more confident and visionary, not constantly playing it safe just to avoid anxiety.\n\nSo, yes, I’m functioning, but I don’t feel as free or confident in my work as I used to. And that’s been weighing on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these worries and checking behaviors are in line with your own values and how you want to approach your work?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually one of the things that bothers me most about it, because it feels like it’s pulling me away from how I want to work.\n\nI care about doing careful, polished work — that part is in line with my values. I don’t want to be sloppy, and I don’t want to send something out with obvious mistakes. But the checking has gone past that normal professional standard, I think. It’s not just “I’m being thorough.” It’s more like I’m holding onto things because I’m scared that if I miss one small issue, it’ll reflect badly on me or make people question whether I’m ready for the next step.\n\nAnd that’s where it feels really out of sync with who I want to be as a designer. I want to be more decisive, more creative, more willing to take a strong point of view. Especially because I’m hoping to move toward a creative director role, and I know that requires confidence and judgment — not just making sure every tiny thing is safe. But lately I’ll catch myself choosing the less risky design option, not because I think it’s better, but because it feels easier to defend or less likely to have some hidden problem I overlooked.\n\nSo I’m still getting my work done, but it doesn’t feel like I’m approaching it the way I want to. Missing internal deadlines because I keep checking, and holding back on bolder ideas because I’m afraid of damaging my chances… that feels really frustrating. It feels like the worry is making decisions for me sometimes, instead of my actual creative instincts.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries or checking behaviors are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I’m not sure I can point to anything major, honestly. It’s much more centered around work, or at least that’s where I notice it the most because that’s where the stakes feel clearer to me.\n\nI mean, I probably do little checking things in normal life like anyone does — making sure I have my keys, making sure I sent a message to the right person, rereading something before I send it — but I don’t think it causes me significant distress in the same way. It doesn’t feel like it’s taking over my evenings or making me avoid things outside of work, if that makes sense.\n\nThe distress is really tied to my job and the sense that a mistake could affect how people see me professionally, or whether I’m ready for more responsibility. Outside of that, I haven’t noticed the same pattern causing a big problem. At least not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these nagging thoughts and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not completely sure. It’s hard for me to put an exact start date on it, because it didn’t feel like something that suddenly appeared one day. It’s more like… at some point I realized I was spending more time circling back over things than I used to.\n\nI can say it’s been long enough that it feels like a pattern now, not just one stressful week or one particular project. But if you asked me, like, “Was it six months, a year, two years?” I don’t know that I could answer confidently without really thinking back through specific work periods.\n\nI think I noticed it gradually — first as being extra careful, then maybe being “thorough,” and then eventually realizing, okay, I’m not just checking because it’s useful, I’m checking because I feel unsettled if I don’t. But I don’t have a clean timeline for it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your relationships with friends or family due to your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not in a big, obvious way, I don’t think. I haven’t had anyone say, “You’re not present,” or “You’re always distracted by work,” or anything like that. My friends and family aren’t really involved in the checking itself, because that mostly happens when I’m working.\n\nI guess there are moments where I’m mentally still at work, like if I’m thinking about whether I missed something or whether something I sent was okay, I might be a little less relaxed or a little quieter. But I don’t know that I’d say it’s damaged my relationships. It’s more internal for me — like I’m carrying that worry around — but I don’t think it’s caused major conflict or anything.\n\nSo… I’d say maybe it affects my mood sometimes, but I haven’t noticed a significant relationship impact.\n\nDoctor: Are there any work tasks or projects that you've been putting off or avoiding altogether because of your fear of making mistakes?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m avoiding whole projects altogether, exactly. I mean, I’m still getting my work done, so it’s not like I’m refusing assignments or just not turning things in. But… yes, there are definitely tasks where I sort of slow down or put off moving them forward because I’m worried I’ll miss something.\n\nIt happens especially with pieces that feel more visible, or where there’s more room for judgment — like design concepts that are supposed to be a little more ambitious. I’ll start second-guessing whether the idea is too risky, whether I’ve overlooked some detail, whether someone will catch something and think, “Why didn’t she notice that?” And then instead of pushing the stronger or more interesting direction, I’ll sometimes stay safer than I probably need to.\n\nI’ve also started missing some internal deadlines, not client deadlines so much, but the check-in points or the timelines I set with the team. It’s frustrating because I’m not missing them because I’m careless — it’s almost the opposite. I’m spending too much time reviewing and adjusting and making sure nothing is wrong, and then I’m late anyway.\n\nAnd I think the part that bothers me most is that I’m trying to move toward more leadership, eventually a creative director role, and I worry that if I make mistakes now, people will see me as not ready. But then the checking and hesitating kind of creates its own problem, because I’m not always showing the boldness or confidence I know that role would require.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your ability to function effectively in your job or in social situations is significantly impaired because of these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s significantly impairing me socially. Socially, it’s more that I might be distracted or still thinking about work, but I’m not avoiding people or having major problems with friends or family because of it.\n\nAt work, though… yes, there is some impact. I’m still functioning, and I’m still getting my actual work done, so part of me hesitates to call it “significant,” because I don’t want to make it sound like I’m falling apart or unable to do my job. But it is interfering more than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe main thing is that I’ve started missing internal deadlines — not usually the final client deadlines, but the check-ins, the drafts, the points where I’m supposed to show progress to the team. And it’s because I keep circling back, reviewing things, tweaking, checking for mistakes, trying to make sure I haven’t overlooked something. So I’m trying to be careful, but then the process gets slower and heavier than it should.\n\nAnd I also notice I hold back on bolder design ideas sometimes. Like if there’s a concept that could be stronger or more interesting, I’ll start thinking, “What if there’s some flaw I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” And because I’m trying to move toward more of a creative director role eventually, I worry that a visible mistake would make people think I’m not ready. So yes, I’m still doing my job, but I do think these worries are affecting how confidently and efficiently I do it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to your work or making mistakes?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not that I’ve noticed in the same way. The thoughts that feel intrusive are pretty much tied to work, especially layouts or client files.\n\nIt’s like I’ll get this nagging thought that I missed some tiny mistake, or that I sent the wrong version to a client, even when I can look at it and think, “Okay, logically this is probably fine.” But then my mind doesn’t really let it go. It keeps circling back to it, like there’s this little hook in my brain saying, “Are you sure? What if you didn’t check that one part?” \n\nBut outside of that work mistake theme, I can’t think of other recurring intrusive thoughts that stand out. It’s really mostly that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your personal life where you feel like you're not enjoying things as much as you used to because of these worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m not enjoying my personal life as much, exactly. I mean, I still enjoy things when I’m actually present for them — dinner with my husband, seeing friends, watching something, going for a walk, that kind of thing. It’s not like everything feels flat or pointless.\n\nBut sometimes the work worries sort of follow me home, if that makes sense. I’ll be doing something normal and then I’ll get pulled back into thinking about whether I missed something or whether I should check something again. So in those moments I’m less relaxed than I want to be. It’s more like I’m distracted or preoccupied, not that I’ve lost interest in my life.\n\nI don’t think there’s a whole area of my personal life that I’ve stopped enjoying. It’s more intermittent — like the worry intrudes on my attention, and then I have to work to come back to whatever I’m doing. So… no, not in a major way, but it does take some of the ease out of things sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or hobbies outside of work that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a clear way. I’m trying to think through it because I don’t want to just automatically say no if there’s some pattern I haven’t noticed.\n\nI still do the things I normally like doing outside of work. I still spend time with my husband, I still see friends, I still go out or do low-key things at home. I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped a hobby because of the worries. It’s more that sometimes I’m mentally not as fully there as I’d like to be, but I’m still doing the activities.\n\nLike, if anything is happening less often, it’s probably more because of normal life and being busy or tired, not because I’m avoiding it due to the work worries. I don’t think there’s a specific hobby where I’d say, “I gave that up because I was too worried about checking things.” It’s more occasional distraction than actually stopping activities.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your overall quality of life is being significantly impacted by these worries and checking behaviors?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say my whole quality of life is significantly impacted, like I’m falling apart or not functioning. I am still getting my work done, and from the outside I probably look like I’m handling things pretty normally.\n\nBut… yes, there is an impact, especially professionally. It’s not just annoying thoughts anymore. I’ve started missing some internal deadlines because I spend too long checking or second-guessing things before I’m willing to send them along. And I know those internal deadlines aren’t the same as a client deadline, but they still matter. People notice if I’m slower or if I’m holding things up.\n\nThe other part that bothers me is that I’ve noticed I’m playing it safer with my design choices. Like, I’ll have a bolder idea, something more creative or maybe more leadership-level, and then I talk myself out of it because I’m afraid there’ll be something I overlooked or some flaw I didn’t catch. So I’ll choose the cleaner, safer option because it feels less risky.\n\nAnd that worries me because I do want to move toward a creative director role eventually. I don’t want to be seen as careless, obviously, but I also don’t want to become someone who’s so cautious that I stop taking creative risks. So I guess it’s mild in the sense that I’m still functioning, but it’s affecting the way I work and the way I see my future at work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any parts of your life outside of work where you feel like these worries are causing you significant distress or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, I don’t think it’s causing significant distress in the same way. I mean, I can get annoyed with myself if I’m trying to relax and my mind drifts back to work, but I wouldn’t say it’s making it hard for me to do the things I need or want to do in my personal life.\n\nI’m still going out, still spending time with my husband, still keeping up with normal life stuff. It doesn’t feel like I’m avoiding personal activities because of the worry. It’s more like the worry sometimes tags along in the background, which is frustrating, but not disabling.\n\nSo I guess my answer is no, not significantly outside of work. It’s mostly the work side where I notice it actually affecting my choices and pace.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your relationships with your husband, friends, or family are suffering because of your preoccupation with work mistakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think they’re really suffering, no. At least not in a way that I’ve noticed, and my husband hasn’t said anything like, “You’re not present,” or “This is becoming a problem.” I mean, I’m sure there are moments where I’m more distracted than I’d like to be, especially if something at work is still kind of sitting in my head, but I don’t think it’s damaging the relationships.\n\nWith my husband, I might talk something through more than I need to sometimes, or ask for reassurance in a kind of roundabout way, but it’s not like we’re fighting about it or I’m withdrawing from him. And with friends and family, I’m still showing up. I still text people back, I still make plans, I still enjoy being with them once I’m there.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not suffering. Maybe occasionally I’m not as mentally available as I’d like, but I haven’t seen it create real strain in those relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your worries or habits are causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, not really in a significant way. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about things, but when I think about actual interference — like not being able to do normal life things or avoiding situations — I don’t really see that happening much outside of my job.\n\nThe main place it shows up is still work. I’m technically getting my work done, so I don’t want to overstate it like I’m completely unable to function, because that wouldn’t be true. But I have started missing internal deadlines because I get caught checking and rechecking or hesitating before I send something along. And those aren’t always huge deadlines, but they do affect the flow of projects, and I worry people will start to see me as slow or overly cautious.\n\nAnd then the other thing is that it’s affecting my choices creatively. I’ll have an idea that’s stronger or more interesting, and then I start thinking, “What if there’s something wrong with it that I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” So I back away from it and choose something safer. That part bothers me a lot, because I want to keep growing. I’d like to move toward a creative director role someday, and I’m afraid this pattern could hold me back.\n\nSo outside of work, I’d say no, not significantly. And with friends and family, I’m still showing up. I still text people back, I still make plans, I still enjoy being with them once I’m there.\n\nSo… I guess I’d say no, not suffering. Maybe occasionally I’m not as mentally available as I’d like, but I haven’t seen it create real strain in those relationships.\n\nDoctor: Are there any situations outside of work where you feel like your worries or habits are causing you significant distress or interfering with your ability to function?\n\nPatient: Outside of work, not really in a significant way. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about things, but when I think about actual interference — like not being able to do normal life things or avoiding situations — I don’t really see that happening much outside of my job.\n\nThe main place it shows up is still work. I’m technically getting my work done, so I don’t want to overstate it like I’m completely unable to function, because that wouldn’t be true. But I have started missing internal deadlines because I get caught checking and rechecking or hesitating before I send something along. And those aren’t always huge deadlines, but they do affect the flow of projects, and I worry people will start to see me as slow or overly cautious.\n\nAnd then the other thing is that it’s affecting my choices creatively. I’ll have an idea that’s stronger or more interesting, and then I start thinking, “What if there’s something wrong with it that I’m not seeing?” or “What if this makes me look careless?” So I back away from it and choose something safer. That part bothers me a lot, because I want to keep growing. I’d like to move toward a creative director role someday, and I’m afraid this pattern could hold me back.\n\nSo outside of work, I’d say no, not significantly. But professionally, yes — it’s starting to interfere, even if I’m still managing overall.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries and checking behaviors been present for more than a year?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to date it, honestly. I don’t have a clear moment where I can say, “This started then,” or “It’s definitely been exactly this long.”\n\nI think some version of being careful or second-guessing myself has probably been around for a while, but the part where I’m really noticing the checking and the worry as a pattern… I’d have to think about that more. It feels like it became more noticeable gradually, rather than suddenly.\n\nSo I don’t want to say yes to “more than a year” if I’m not certain. I’d say I’m not sure. It may have been, but I’d need to look back and sort of piece it together."
  }
